```text
68	% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===
69	\clearpage
70	\section*{Limitations}
71	
72	\paragraph{Constraint scope.}
73	CF impact is model-dependent: for well-calibrated models (Qwen, Llama; DR\,$\leq$\,1\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.
74	CF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\,pp). CF$\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).
75	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.
76	CF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.
77	Entity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\,pp for Qwen vs.\ $-$0.64\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\% vs.\ 6.8\%).
78	On 70B DocRED LoRA, parse failure rises modestly under S1 (10.0\%\,$\to$\,12.5\%) due to generation timeouts on long documents rather than JSON format errors; SciERC (shorter documents) shows 0\% parse failure in both settings.
79	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
80	Schema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.
81	Post-hoc type consensus re-voting (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}) reduces CF violations by 18--92\% depending on model and dataset, confirming that first-assignment ordering sensitivity is a primary CF source; however, integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.
82	
83	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
84	Zero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\% compared to $\sim$45\% for fine-tuned systems.
85	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).
86	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified; the observed relationship between schema restrictiveness and enforcement difficulty is based on only three schemas.
87	Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%, R-F1 within $\pm$0.8\,pp; EE: RoV$\to$0\%, AC$\leq$0.8\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
88	WikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).
89	DocRED experiments evaluate on the development set, as test set annotations are not publicly available.
90	All main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.
91	
92	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
93	Scalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\,s; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.
94	Our approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.
95	Continuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.
96	This assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \texttt{Protein} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Complex} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.
97	70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms that INT4 does not affect constraint enforcement (DR remains 0\%; Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}), providing indirect evidence, though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.
98	Our evaluation is limited to English-language datasets.
99	The grammar layer (CFG compilation via XGrammar) is tokenizer-agnostic, applying unchanged to any BPE or SentencePiece tokenizer including those trained on CJK-heavy corpora.
100	The semantic layer's continuation masking is likewise language-independent in mechanism: prefix matching over type name tokens operates identically regardless of script, though CJK entity names produce more fragmented tokenizations under byte-level BPE (2--3$\times$ more tokens per entity than English equivalents), increasing the number of masking steps per type field without affecting correctness.
101	Validation on Chinese IE benchmarks (e.g., DuIE, CLUENER) remains future work.
102	Fine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\%\,$\to$\,0\%; Appendix~\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.
103	The two-pass strategy (S2) exhibits scale-dependent degradation: at 70B, S2 reduces R-F1 by 8.45\,pp while 8B models show comparable S1/S2 performance, suggesting optimal constraint granularity varies with model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:70b-ablation}).
104	
105	
106	% === Ethics Statement ===
107	\section*{Ethics Statement}
```

Converting DR violations to CF represents a practical advantage since CF violations can be resolved post-hoc through constraint resolution, whereas DR violations require re-extraction. Zero-shot evaluation establishes the prevalence of violations and how constraints interact, while fine-tuning shows these issues can be largely eliminated in practice.

For the fine-tuning expansion, I need to incorporate the fallback frequency analysis and model dependence—comparing Qwen versus Llama performance on this metric. I should also integrate the CF quantification into the ablation discussion, noting that under greedy decoding, CF violations constitute a significant portion of residual violations on SciERC. ```text
1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	With LoRA, beam search ($k{=}4$)~\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on both SciERC and DocRED at both 8B and 70B scales (R-F1 43--47; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}), yielding two deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\% latency, DR${<}$0.1\%, CF${\leq}$1.4\%) and beam S1 (1.54$\times$ latency, all violations${\to}$0\%; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
11	
12	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200
13	\paragraph{70B-class validation.}
14	\label{sec:70b-results}
15	On Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4),\footnote{All 70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization. An 8B ablation confirms that INT4 does not affect constraint enforcement (DR remains 0\%; Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}).} zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets (SciERC 6.41\%\,$\to$\,0\%, DocRED 14.98\%\,$\to$\,0\%; R-F1 11--13).
16	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_ablation (grammar_only, semantic_only, full_s1)
17	A four-way ablation reveals scale-dependent layer interaction: semantic-only achieves 84\% DR reduction vs.\ 8B's 24\% (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), though grammar remains necessary for RV elimination and CF control (semantic-only CF${=}$76).
18	% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained
19	LoRA confirms composability: DR\,$\to$\,0\% on both datasets (R-F1 44.53, 43.95; $|\Delta| \leq 0.20$\,pp; CF 0.18--1.33\%); cross-family generalization at 70B scale remains to be verified.
20	% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_docred_200_beam4_s1
21	Beam ($k{=}4$) reduces DocRED parse failures (12.5\%\,$\to$\,0.5\%, R-F1 $+$4.19\,pp); SciERC controlled comparison isolates pure beam gain at $+$0.28\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:70b-ablation}).
22	
23	% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc
24	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
25	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}
26	Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}; vLLM \texttt{guided\_json}~\cite{kwon2023vllm} confirms equivalence (DR${=}$15.26\%; GCD-equiv.\ row, Appendix~\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).
27	The ${\sim}$15\% residual DR is the gap our semantic layer addresses (94\% total reduction); trie-only constraints without JSON enforcement yield 9--51\% parse failure (Appendix~\ref{app:trie-baseline}).
28	
```

U: unconstrained; S1: full two-layer.\nParse failures: Llama DocRED 9.1\\%; others $\\leq$1.1\\%.\n$^\\dagger$Mistral: poor zero-shot calibration causes pervasive type inconsistencies.\nF1 $\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model results.}\nRV and ET reach $\\leq$0.03\\% for all models on both datasets.\nDR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; R-F1 improves in 5/6 settings.\nCF direction depends on calibration: S1 introduces CF for calibrated models (up to 12.02\\%) but reduces it for Mistral (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%); fine-tuning resolves both (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\nPost-hoc filtering achieves DR${=}$0\\% but at 15--74\\% rejection rates (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset validation on DocRED.}\n\\label{sec:docred-results}\nDocRED (96 relations, 14$\\times$ SciERC scale) validates the pattern under a larger, more restrictive ontology (Table~\\ref{tab:docred-ablation}).\nS1 reduces DR from 18.46\\% to 10.21\\% ($\\downarrow$45\\%), eliminates RV (29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%) and ET, with R-F1 $+$0.50\\,pp ($p < 0.001$).\nThe lower DR reduction (45\\% vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC) reflects DocRED's tighter constraints, which trigger more grammar-first fallback (\\S\\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}).\nGrammar-only \\emph{increases} DR (18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,26.17\\%) via \\textit{latent violation surfacing}: grammar forces OOV$\\to$valid relation mapping, exposing type violations previously masked by vocabulary errors---a distinct cross-dataset confirmation of the scale-dependent layer interaction.\nFine-tuning resolves residual DR entirely (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}; residual decomposition in Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\n"
}

1	% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)
2	% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments

7	\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}
8	\label{sec:scierc-results}
9	
10	Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.
11

62	\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).
63	$\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\ unconstrained; Tot.\ = DR + CF; bold = best (CF excluded).

71	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
72	%  (a) Core result
73	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
74	\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}
75	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093
76	% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824
77	S1 reduces DR from 15.37\% to 0.93\% ($\downarrow$94\%) with unchanged R-F1 ($p{=}0.58$), confirming the \emph{empirical enabling dependency}: grammar-only eliminates RV/ET but achieves only 3\% DR reduction; semantic-only reaches 24\%; their composition yields 94\% (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).
78	Total type-violation burden decreases from 15.45\% to 10.83\%, reflecting \emph{violation conversion} (CF 0.08\%\,$\to$\,9.90\%) that fine-tuning resolves (Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
79	S2 achieves comparable DR but lower R-F1; we adopt S1 (per-relation patterns in Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
80	This DR-to-CF conversion represents a severity downgrade: DR violations introduce ontologically invalid type pairs (irrecoverable without re-extraction), while CF violations---individually valid triples with cross-triple inconsistency---are amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\%; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
81	
82	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
83	%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning

86	%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,
87	%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
88	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
89	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
90	\label{sec:composability}
91	% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json, w5_lora_scierc_*, w8_*
92	Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% with no significant $\Delta$R-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95\% CIs $\subseteq [-0.69, {+}0.25]$\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\% false positives; Appendix~\ref{app:downstream-impact}).
93	Under fine-tuning, grammar becomes a no-op ($\Delta$DR$<$0.2\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole DR defense (39--69\% reduction; Appendix~\ref{app:ft-ablation}); three relations with $\kappa \geq 0.36$ retain DR, all eliminated by S1 (Appendix~\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).
94	CF drops from $+$9.82\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (63.3\%; Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types (R-F1 43--47; \S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
95

135	%  exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis
136	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
137	\paragraph{Downstream impact.}
138	\label{sec:downstream-impact}
139	Among constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\% match gold labels (Appendix~\ref{app:lucky-violations}); error analysis of 514 false negatives confirms only one (0.2\%) was blocked by constraints, with dominant sources (relation confusion, entity type polysemy) schema-valid and beyond constraint reach (Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}).
140	Across fine-tuned settings, 36 residual DR violations decompose into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and relation/direction errors (28\%); the irreducible floor is $\sim$3 violations (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).

Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types (R-F1 43--47; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  CF quantification (MF1 response)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Cross-field violation analysis.}\nUnder greedy S1, CF constitutes the dominant residual: 155 of 245 violations on fine-tuned SciERC (63.9\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\\%) and \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{Material} (12.3\\%).\nThis reflects the architectural limitation of per-triple independent enforcement: when multiple triples reference the same entity, the first-seen type registration (\\S\\ref{sec:entity-registry}) may conflict with subsequent type requirements.\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all CF violations by exploring alternative type assignments, but at 1.54$\\times$ latency (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results})."
}

DR\\,$\\leq$\\,1\\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.\nCF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.\nEntity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\\,pp for Qwen vs.\\ $-$0.64\\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\\% vs.\\ 6.8\\%).\nOn 70B DocRED LoRA, parse failure rises modestly under S1 (10.0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12.5\\%) due to generation timeouts on long documents rather than JSON format errors; SciERC (shorter documents) shows 0\\% parse failure in both settings.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nSchema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.\nPost-hoc type consensus re-voting (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}) reduces CF violations by 18--92\\% depending on model and dataset, confirming that first-assignment ordering sensitivity is a primary CF source; however, integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\\% compared to $\\sim$45\\% for fine-tuned systems.\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified; the observed relationship between schema restrictiveness and enforcement difficulty is based on only three schemas.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).\nDocRED experiments evaluate on the development set, as test set annotations are not publicly available.\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nScalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\\,s; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.\nOur approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.\nContinuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.\nThis assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms that INT4 does not affect constraint enforcement (DR remains 0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), providing indirect evidence, though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.\nOur evaluation is limited to English-language datasets.\nThe grammar layer (CFG compilation via XGrammar) is tokenizer-agnostic, applying unchanged to any BPE or SentencePiece tokenizer including those trained on CJK-heavy corpora.\nThe semantic layer's continuation masking is likewise language-independent in mechanism: prefix matching over type name tokens operates identically regardless of script, though CJK entity names produce more fragmented tokenizations under byte-level BPE (2--3$\\times$ more tokens per entity than English equivalents), increasing the number of masking steps per type field without affecting correctness.\nValidation on Chinese IE benchmarks (e.g., DuIE, CLUENER) remains future work.\nFine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.\nThe two-pass strategy (S2) exhibits scale-dependent degradation: at 70B, S2 reduces R-F1 by 8.45\\,pp while 8B models show comparable S1/S2 performance, suggesting optimal constraint granularity varies with model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:70b-ablation}).\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_70b_ablation}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_power_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_downstream_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_high_kappa_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_order_invariance}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\\input{appendix_quantization}\n\\input{appendix_gollie_comparison}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

DR\\,$\\leq$\\,1\\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.\nCF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.\nEntity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\\,pp for Qwen vs.\\ $-$0.64\\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\\% vs.\\ 6.8\\%).\nOn 70B DocRED LoRA, parse failure rises modestly under S1 (10.0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12.5\\%) due to generation timeouts on long documents rather than JSON format errors; SciERC (shorter documents) shows 0\\% parse failure in both settings.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nSchema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.\nPost-hoc type consensus re-voting (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}) reduces CF violations by 18--92\\% depending on model and dataset, confirming that first-assignment ordering sensitivity is a primary CF source; however, integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\\% compared to $\\sim$45\\% for fine-tuned systems.\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified; the observed relationship between schema restrictiveness and enforcement difficulty is based on only three schemas.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).\nDocRED experiments evaluate on the development set, as test set annotations are not publicly available.\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nScalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\\,s; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.\nOur approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.\nContinuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.\nThis assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms that INT4 does not affect constraint enforcement (DR remains 0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), providing indirect evidence, though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.\nOur evaluation is limited to English-language datasets.\nThe grammar layer (CFG compilation via XGrammar) is tokenizer-agnostic, applying unchanged to any BPE or SentencePiece tokenizer including those trained on CJK-heavy corpora.\nThe semantic layer's continuation masking is likewise language-independent in mechanism: prefix matching over type name tokens operates identically regardless of script, though CJK entity names produce more fragmented tokenizations under byte-level BPE (2--3$\\times$ more tokens per entity than English equivalents), increasing the number of masking steps per type field without affecting correctness.\nValidation on Chinese IE benchmarks (e.g., DuIE, CLUENER) remains future work.\nFine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.\nThe two-pass strategy (S2) exhibits scale-dependent degradation: at 70B, S2 reduces R-F1 by 8.45\\,pp while 8B models show comparable S1/S2 performance, suggesting optimal constraint granularity varies with model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:70b-ablation}).\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_70b_ablation}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_power_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_downstream_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_high_kappa_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_order_invariance}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\\input{appendix_quantization}\n\\input{appendix_gollie_comparison}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 41--47\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent empirical enabling dependency: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.54$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\nWe make a systematic engineering contribution with theoretical grounding:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework: we formalize a well-known intuition that cross-field type constraints require runtime state beyond context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement at each level (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas of varying restrictiveness with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item A comprehensive evaluation across 4 models spanning 8B--70B, 2 deployment precisions (FP16/INT4), and 2 relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), with a preliminary event extraction pilot: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\%; zero-shot ablation reveals an empirical enabling dependency (neither layer alone exceeds 24\\% DR reduction at 8B; 84\\% at 70B; \\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}); DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); beam eliminates all violations; a preliminary pilot study on WikiEvents (n${=}$20 test documents) validates event extraction applicability (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 41--47\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent empirical enabling dependency: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.54$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\nWe make a systematic engineering contribution with theoretical grounding:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework: we formalize a well-known intuition that cross-field type constraints require runtime state beyond context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement at each level (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas of varying restrictiveness with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item A comprehensive evaluation across 4 models spanning 8B--70B, 2 deployment precisions (FP16/INT4), and 2 relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), with a preliminary event extraction pilot: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\%; zero-shot ablation reveals an empirical enabling dependency (neither layer alone exceeds 24\\% DR reduction at 8B; 84\\% at 70B; \\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}); DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); beam eliminates all violations; a preliminary pilot study on WikiEvents (n${=}$20 test documents) validates event extraction applicability (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

          " OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).",
          " LoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.54$\\times$ latency).",

    "originalFile": "% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)\n% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,\n%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema\n\n\\label{sec:violation_landscape}\n\nWe evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: greedy S1 (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, $+$10\\% latency) and beam S1 (all violations\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, 1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}--\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) serves as a diagnostic probe for the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 41--47\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).\nRE type constraints are extracted from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) derive from the KAIROS ontology.\nAll constraints are schema-level, independent of evaluation splits.\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nLoRA hyperparameters were selected on SciERC development data and transferred to DocRED without further tuning; DocRED development documents were used for evaluation only.\nFour violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), \\textbf{CF} (conflicting types across triples).\nBaseline DR: 15.37\\% (SciERC), 18.46\\% (DocRED); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\n",

    "originalFile": "% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)\n% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,\n%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema\n\n\\label{sec:violation_landscape}\n\nWe evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: greedy S1 (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, $+$10\\% latency) and beam S1 (all violations\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, 1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}) serves as a diagnostic probe for the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 41--47\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).\nRE type constraints are extracted from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) derive from the KAIROS ontology.\nAll constraints are schema-level, independent of evaluation splits.\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nLoRA hyperparameters were selected on SciERC development data and transferred to DocRED without further tuning; DocRED development documents were used for evaluation only.\nFour violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), \\textbf{CF} (conflicting types across triples).\nBaseline DR: 15.37\\% (SciERC), 18.46\\% (DocRED); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\n",
